# Questions not found in DSAT_REDUX_MD

**669 of 1285** crackap.com questions in `sat_verbal.db` have no matching question in the DSAT_REDUX_MD official verbal question bank (neither an exact nor a near-duplicate passage match). These are either unique to crackap.com's practice sets or paraphrased beyond what text similarity matching can detect.

## Summary by category

| Category | Unmatched questions |
|---|---|
| Words in Context | 117 |
| Student Notes | 65 |
| Command of Evidence | 57 |
| Command of Evidence: Quantitative | 55 |
| Inferences | 52 |
| Textual Evidence | 43 |
| Main Idea | 42 |
| Logical Transition | 39 |
| Conventions of Punctuation | 39 |
| Verb Tense | 31 |
| Details | 31 |
| Rhetorical Purpose | 26 |
| Poetry | 23 |
| Nouns | 16 |
| Cross-text Connections | 12 |
| Pronouns | 10 |
| Adjectives/Adverbs | 7 |
| Elliptical Sentence | 4 |


## Words in Context (117)

### Question #358 (test 38, Q9)

**Passage:**

> People did not suddenly learn to use fire, but did so slowly over time with countless advances offset by  ________  periods.

**Question:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical and precise word or phrase?

**Choices:**
- A. explosive
- B. fertile
- C. predictable
- D. regressive

**Correct answer: D**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-358-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #428 (test 45, Q9)

Citing the data in the table, a journalist claims the population estimates from the United Nations (UN) most likely included people outside the cities proper.

**Question:** Which choice best describes data in the table that support the journalist‘s claim?

**Choices:**
- A. The UN estimated Bogota’s population to be at 10,574,000, which is significantly larger than the reported population for the city proper.
- B. The metropolitan area of Bangkok is significantly larger than 1,569 square kilometers.
- C. The reported city proper population of Bogota be significantly larger than the reported city proper population of Houston.
- D. All the city proper areas are below 3,000 square kilometers.

**Correct answer: A**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-580-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #581 (test 61, Q2)

**Passage:**

> In a college course on urban affairs, a student asserts that increased traffic congestion in the 1990s in the United States was present both in very large cities such as New York City, New York, and smaller areas such as Albuquerque, New Mexico, Jacksonville, Florida, and Boise, Idaho; though those smaller area may have been less affected by traffic congestion than very large cities, this congestion also worsened in them over time.

**Question:** Which choice best describes data from the graph that support the student’s claim?

**Choices:**
- A. In at least one of the four urban areas shown, the amount of traffic delay was less than 20 hours per commuter per year at one point between 1990 and 2000.
- B. While the annual number of hours of traffic delay per commuter was always lower in the Boise, Idaho, area than in the New York City, New York, area for each year between 1990 and 2000, the amount of traffic delay rose in both areas during this period.
- C. Even though the amount of traffic delay per commuter per year was greater in the Albuquerque, New Mexico, area than in the New York City, New York, area throughout the period from 1990 to 2000, the amounts were nearly identical in 1996.
- D. In 1992, the amount of traffic delay in the New York City, New York, area was less than 30 hours per commuter per year.

**Correct answer: B**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-581-answer-and-explanation.html_

- C. moving between the ground and the trees would have been difficult without bipedalism.

### Question #949 (test 100, Q1)

> While researching a topic, a student has taken the following notes:

> • She interviewed Standard Oil Company executives, oil industry workers, and public officials.
> • She examined thousands of pages of the company’s internal comMônications, including letters and financial records.
> • Her book The History of the Standard Oil Company (1904) exposed the company’s unfair business practices.

- B. As part of her investigation of the Standard Oil Company, muckraker Ida Tarbell conducted interviews.
- C. Published in 1904, muckraker Ida Tarbell’s book The History of the Standard Oil Company exposed the company’s unfair business practices.

### Question #954 (test 100, Q6)

**Passage:**

> While researching a topic, a student has taken the following notes:
> • About 100 million years ago, a group of trendsetting moths started flying during the day rather than at night.
> • The moths took advantage of nectar-rich flowers that had co-evolved with bees.
> • This single event led to the evolution of all butterflies.
> • Scientists have known the precise timing of this event since 2019 when a large-scale analysis of D.N.A. discounted an earlier hypothesis that pressure from bats prompted butterflies’ evolution after dinosaurs’ extinction.
> • Researchers created the world’s largest butterfly tree of life, which they used as a guide to trace the evolution of butterflies through time in a four-dimensional puzzle that led back to North and Central America.
> • According to their results, published this Monday in the journal?Nature Ecology and Evolution, this is where the first butterflies took flight.
> • In pretty much every family of butterflies, bean plants came out to be the ancestral hosts.

**Question:** The student wants to present their study and findings. Which choice most effectively uses relevant information from the notes to accomplish this goal?

**Choices:**
- A. A group of moths started flying during the day about 100 million years ago, feeding on nectar-rich flowers that co-evolved with bees, and this event led to the evolution of all butterflies.
- B. Scientists have known the precise timing of this event since 2019, and according to their research, the first butterflies took flight in North and Central America.
- C. Researchers traced the evolution of butterflies through time using the world’s largest butterfly tree of life. They found that the first butterflies took flight in North and Central America, with bean plants as the ancestral hosts in most butterfly families.
- D. The first butterflies evolved around 100 million years ago, and most butterfly families have bean plants as their ancestral hosts.

**Correct answer: C**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-954-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #955 (test 100, Q7)

**Passage:**

> While researching a topic, a student has taken the following notes:
> • One hypothesis is that trauma survivors often grow up to have troubled relationships as adults, and the resulting lack of social support, in turn, is what cuts their life short.
> • But the new findings paint a different picture of the causal pathway involved in baboons and offer some hope.
> • In the study, the researchers looked at how early life experiences and adult social connections affected long-term survival in 199 female baboons closely monitored at Amboseli between 1983 and 2019.
> • Drawing on 36 years of data, a new study of 199 baboons in southern Kenya finds that adversity early in life can take years off the lifespan, but strong social bonds with other baboons in adulthood can help get them back.
> • Baboons who formed stronger social bonds -- measured by how often they groomed with their closest friends -- added 2.2 years to their lives, no matter what hardships they faced when they were younger.
> • The findings appear May 17 in the journal?Science Advances.

**Question:** The student wants to present their study and findings. Which choice most effectively uses relevant information from the notes to accomplish this goal?

**Choices:**
- A. A study found that early-life adversity can reduce the lifespan of baboons. Still, strong social bonds in adulthood can mitigate this effect, adding 2.2 years to their lives regardless of past hardships.
- B. A study of 199 female baboons in southern Kenya found that adversity early in life can shorten lifespan, but strong social bonds in adulthood can help counteract this effect, with baboons who formed stronger social bonds adding 2.2 years to their lives, according to research published in Science Advances.
- C. The hypothesis that trauma survivors have troubled relationships as adults, leading to a lack of social support and shortened lifespan, is challenged by new findings in baboons that offer hope for better outcomes.
- D. Adult friendships can triumph over childhood trauma, even in baboons.

**Correct answer: B**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-955-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #956 (test 100, Q8)

**Passage:**

> While researching a topic, a student has taken the following notes:
> • Electricity production in nuclear power plants generates radioactive waste as a by-product.
> • The waste is often placed in canisters (containers), but it remains radioactive for thousands of years, emitting invisible radiation harmful to humans and other living things.
> • Scientists are currently discussing several strategies for safely storing canisters.
> • The canisters must be stored for thousands of years until the waste loses its radioactivity.
> • One option is to store the canisters in permanent structures above ground.
> • Another option is storing the canisters in boreholes—long, vertical holes dug deep into the Earth.
> • Another solution is to store the canisters under the large, uninhabited surface of solid ice that has covered Antarctica for thousands of years.

**Question:** The student wants to introduce safely storing the canisters with radioactive waste to an unfamiliar audience. Which choice most effectively uses relevant information from the notes to accomplish this goal?

**Choices:**
- A. Nuclear power plant waste is radioactive and can harm living things, so scientists are discussing safe storage options such as above-ground structures, boreholes, and under the ice in Antarctica.
- B. Safely storing canisters with radioactive waste can be achieved through various methods, such as permanent structures above ground, boreholes, and storage under Antarctica’s uninhabited surface of solid ice.
- C. Safely storing canisters with radioactive waste is important to prevent harm to human health and the environment from radiation exposure.
- D. Nuclear power plants produce radioactive waste that needs to be safely stored for thousands of years due to its harmful effects on humans and the environment.

**Correct answer: B**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-956-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #958 (test 101, Q1)

**Passage:**

> While researching a topic, a student has taken the following notes:
> • Dengue is a viral infection that is transmitted by mosquitoes.
> • Symptoms of dengue fever include high fever, severe headache, joint and muscle pain, and rash.
> • Dengue can progress to a more severe form called dengue hemorrhagic fever, which can be life-threatening.
> • There is no specific treatment for dengue, but supportive care can help manage symptoms and prevent complications.
> • Prevention measures for dengue include avoiding mosquito bites, eliminating mosquito breeding sites, and using mosquito repellent.

**Question:** The student wants to introduce the study and its findings. Which choice most effectively uses relevant information from the notes to accomplish this goal?

**Choices:**
- A. Dengue is a viral infection transmitted by mosquitoes, with symptoms including high fever, headache, joint and muscle pain, and rash, and can progress to a life-threatening hemorrhagic fever but can be managed through supportive care and prevented by avoiding mosquito bites and breeding sites and using repellent.
- B. Dengue fever is a mosquito-borne viral disease prevalent in tropical and subtropical regions and caused by four distinct serotypes of the dengue virus.
- C. Dengue fever can lead to a range of symptoms from mild to severe, including high fever, severe headache, joint and muscle pain, nausea, vomiting, rash, and in severe cases, dengue hemorrhagic fever or dengue shock syndrome, which can be life-threatening.
- D. Prevention is key for dengue, as no specific treatment is available; supportive care can help manage symptoms and prevent complications. Prevention can be achieved by avoiding mosquito bites, eliminating breeding sites, and using repellent.

### Question #959 (test 101, Q2)

- B. Plastic debris, which is the accumulation of plastic waste materials in the environment, including land, water, and air, is a major environmental issue threatening wildlife and ecosystems.
- C. Plastic debris, which can take hundreds of years to decompose and can be found in many forms, including microplastics, which are tiny particles of plastic that organisms can ingest, leads to large amounts of waste in the environment.
- D. Plastic debris can harm marine life and release harmful chemicals into the environment, negatively impacting both animals and humans.

### Question #962 (test 101, Q5)

> • Typically, the resistance is free weights or weight machines, but you can also use resistance bands and your body weight.
> • Strength training can burn calories during and after the workout, help counter age-related muscle loss, and fix muscle imbalances or weaknesses affecting your performance.

**Question:** The student wants to compare the effects of cardio and strength training. Which choice most effectively uses relevant information from the notes to accomplish this goal?

**Choices:**
- A. Cardio exercise requires oxygen and includes activities like walking, running, cycling, and swimming; it reduces the risk of cardiovascular disease, helps maintain a healthy weight, relieves stress, and builds endurance.
- B. Strength training involves working against resistance to build strength and muscle, and it can burn calories, counter age-related muscle loss, and fix muscle imbalances.
- C. Cardio primarily improves cardiovascular health, while strength training improves muscular strength and endurance.
- D. Cardio includes walking, running, cycling, and swimming, while strength training includes weight lifting, bodyweight, resistance band, and weight machine exercises.

**Correct answer: C**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-988-answer-and-explanation.html_

- A. Toyota is committed to innovation and has developed groundbreaking technologies, such as the hybrid powertrain used in the Prius.
- B. Toyota has set a goal to reduce its global carbon footprint by 90% by 2050. It has become a trusted brand in the automotive industry with a strong focus on customer satisfaction and quality.
- C. Toyota, a Japanese automaker producing various vehicles from compact cars to luxury S.U.V.s since 1937, has a reputation for producing reliable and durable vehicles, making it a popular choice among consumers worldwide.
- D. Toyota is a Japanese multinational automotive company known for producing a wide range of reliable and durable vehicles since 1937; committed to innovation and sustainability, with a strong focus on customer satisfaction and quality, making it a trusted brand in the automotive industry.

**Correct answer: D**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-1020-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #1021 (test 108, Q1)

**Passage:**

> While researching a topic, a student has taken the following notes:
> • Death Valley is a desert in the southwestern United States.
> • In part of the desert Death Valley called Racetrack Playa, there is an unusual phenomenon known as the sailing stones.
> • The stones weigh up to 300 kilograms.
> • The stones occasionally shift locations, sometimes moving hundreds of meters over a few months.
> • Scientists have recorded strong winds in the playa, and when it rains, the playa's muddy floor becomes slick and slippery.
> • Pulled by gravity, large and thick ice sheets could drag along even heavy stones trapped within them.
> • People rather than natural forces may have moved around the sailing stones.

**Question:** The student wants to introduce the sailing stones to an unfamiliar audience. Which choice most effectively uses relevant information from the notes to accomplish this goal?

**Choices:**
- A. Sailing stones, also known as sliding rocks or moving rocks, are a geological phenomenon in certain remote areas of the world, like the Racetrack Playa in Death Valley National Park.
- B. The sailing stones are a geological phenomenon where rocks move across a dry lake bed, leaving long trails behind them without human or animal intervention.
- C. The sailing stones can move due to a combination of wind, ice, and water, which create a thin layer of ice that allows the rocks to slide across the surface of the dry lake bed.
- D. The sailing stone is a rock found on a dry lake bed, like the Racetrack Playa in Death Valley, known for its ability to move due to a combination of wind, ice, and water creating a thin layer of ice that allows it to slide across the surface.

**Correct answer: D**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-1021-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #1022 (test 108, Q2)

**Passage:**

> While researching a topic, a student has taken the following notes:
> • Koalas are native Australian animals that feed mainly on the leaves of the eucalyptus tree.
> • The koala is a vulnerable species that has recently experienced sharp population declines.
> • Populations of wild koalas will likely continue to decline due to several serious threats.
> • Habitat loss forces koalas into increasingly fragmented patches or small areas of forest surrounded by treeless ground.
> • In their searches for new sources of food, water, and mates, koalas move along the ground and cross busy, high-speed roads that intersect their habitat.
> • Many koalas are seriously injured in dog attacks every year because they cannot climb over fences fast enough to escape.

**Question:** The student wants to introduce the sharp population decline of the koala to an audience familiar with koalas. Which choice most effectively uses relevant information from the notes to accomplish this goal?

**Choices:**
- A. Automobile collisions present a significant danger to koalas.
- B. Fencing is a major obstacle to koalas that live in urban areas.
- C. Loss of suitable habitat, migration hazards, city fences, etc., may be the reasons for the decline of koalas.
- D. There are several reasons for the decline in koala numbers, one of which is that their survival is certainly under threat.

**Correct answer: C**

## Command of Evidence (57)

### Question #729 (test 77, Q1)

**Passage:**

> Disco remains one of the most ridiculed popular music genres of the late twentieth century. But as scholars have argued, the genre is far less superficial than many people believe. Take the case of disco icon Donna Summer: she may have been associated with popular songs about love and heartbreak (subjects hardly unique to disco, by the way), but like many Black women singers before her, much of her music also reflects concerns about community and identity. These concerns are present in many of the genre’s greatest songs, and they generally don’t require much digging to reveal.

**Question:** What does the text most strongly suggest about the disco genre?

**Choices:**
- A. It gave rise to a Black women’s musical tradition that has endured even though the genre itself faded in the late twentieth century.
- B. It has been unjustly ignored by most scholars despite the importance of the themes addressed by many of the genre’s songs.
- C. It has been unfairly dismissed for the inclusion of subject matter that is also found in other musical genres.
- D. It evolved over time from a superficial genre focused on romance to a genre focused on more serious concerns.

**Correct answer: C**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-729-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #732 (test 77, Q4)

**Passage:**

> Kate: The recent decline in numbers of the Tennessee warbler, a North American songbird that migrates each fall to coffee plantations in South America, is due to the elimination of the dense tree cover that formerly was a feature of most South American coffee plantations.
> Scott: The population of the spruce budworm, the warbler’s favorite prey in North America, has been dropping. This is a more likely explanation of the warbler’s decline.

**Question:** Which of the following, if true, most seriously calls Scott’s hypothesis into question?

**Choices:**
- A. The numbers of the Baltimore oriole, a songbird that dose not eat budworms but is as dependent on South American coffee plantations as is the Tennessee warbler, are declining.
- B. The spruce-budworm population has dropped because of a disease that can infect budworms but not Tennessee warblers.
- C. The drop in the population of the spruce budworm is expected to be only temporary.
- D. Many Tennessee warbler have begun migrating in the fall to places other than traditional coffee plantations.

**Correct answer: A**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-732-answer-and-explanation.html_

- B. Machu Picchu is already one of the most popular tourist sites in Peru
- C. Natural weathering will continue to be a more significant cause of the deterioration of Machu Picchu than tourist traffic
- D. The cable car will replace the tour buses whose large wheels and corrosive exhaust at present do significant damage to the site.

### Question #734 (test 77, Q6)

**Passage:**

> In the late 1980’s, the population of sea otters in the North Pacific Ocean began to decline. Of the two plausible explanations for the decline—increased predation by killer whales or disease—disease is the more likely. After all, a concurrent sharp decline in the populations of seals and sea lions was almost certainly caused by a pollution-related disease, which could have spread to sea otters, whereas the population of killer whales did not change noticeably.

**Question:** Which of the following, if true, most seriously weakens the reasoning?

**Choices:**
- A. Killer whales in the North Pacific usually prey on seals and sea lions but will, when this food source is scarce, seek out other prey.
- B. There is no indication that substantial numbers of sea otters migrated to other locations from the North Pacific in the 1980’s.
- C. Along the Pacific coast of North America in the 1980’s, sea otters were absent from many locations where they had been relatively common in former times.
- D. Following the decline in the population of the sea otters, there was an increase in the population of sea urchins, which are sea otters’ main food source.

**Correct answer: A**

### Question #737 (test 77, Q9)

**Passage:**

> Many governments that regularly transfer money to individuals—to provide supplemental incomes for senior citizens, for example—have long done so electronically, but other countries typically have distributed physical money and have only recently developed electronic transfer infrastructure. Researchers studied the introduction of an electronic transfer system in one such location and found that recipients of electronic transfers consumed a different array of foods than recipients of physical transfers of the same amount did. One potential explanation for this result is that individuals conceive of and allocate funds in physical money differently than they conceive of and allocate funds in electronic form.

**Question:** Which finding from the study, if true, would most directly weaken the potential explanation?

**Choices:**
- A. Recipients of electronic transfers typically spent their funds at a slower rate than recipients of physical transfers did.
- B. Some recipients of physical transfers received small amounts of money relatively frequently, while others received large amounts relatively infrequently.
- C. Recipients of physical transfers tended to purchase food about as frequently as recipients of electronic transfers did.
- D. Nearly every recipient of an electronic transfer withdrew the entire amount in physical money shortly after receiving the transfer.

**Correct answer: D**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-737-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #742 (test 78, Q4)

**Passage:**

> Archaeologists have traditionally thought that the rise of agriculture required early farmers to settle down near their crops. But new findings suggest that Catalhoyük, Turkey – a large Neolithic village of such early farmers – was smack in the middle of marshy wetlands. Archaeologist Arlene Rosen’s analysis of fossil remains of wheat and barley found at Catalhoyük indicates that the grain was grown in a dry area. Some experts reject the implication that Catalhoyük’s farmers cultivated distant fields, since large quantities of grain would have had to be transported. However, archaeobotanist Eleni Asouti has shown that the wood used for construction at Catalhoyük grew at least twelve kilometers away from the village.

**Question:** Which finding, if true, would most challenge the "implication" mentioned in the text?

**Choices:**
- A. Catalhoyük’s farmers obtained through trade the wheat and barley that Rosen analyzed.
- B. Catalhoyük’s farmers understood the impact of soil conditions on crop productivity.
- C. Catalhoyük’s farmers alternated on an annual basis the crops they planted.
- D. Catalhoyük’s farmers shared wheat and barley fields with neighboring villages

**Correct answer: A**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-742-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #743 (test 78, Q5)

**Passage:**

> In the 1980s, researchers in Northeastern University visited more than 500 Bahamian islands to survey distributions of vertebrates and arthropods, with special emphasis on lizards and spiders. A study found that spiders were about an order of magnitude denser on no-lizard than lizard islands (adjusted for the positive and negative correlations with area and distance from large landmasses, respectively). What was the mechanism of the (now firmly established) lizard effect on spiders? The obvious one is predation. However, a second is competition for food: Spiders consume prey large in relation to their own size, so lizards and spiders might overlap in prey size well beyond expectations from their relative body sizes alone.

**Question:** Which of the following findings, if true, would best support the explanation presented in the paragraph?

**Choices:**
- A. The total insect biomass consumed by spiders was reduced in lizard enclosures and unenclosed plots compared with the biomass consumed by spiders in lizard removal enclosures.
- B. The survival rate of spiders was significantly higher in lizard removal enclosures than in lizard enclosures and unenclosed plots.
- C. Fewer spider webs were found closer to the ground where lizards might have easier access to them in unenclosed plots and lizard enclosures than in lizard removal enclosures.
- D. Insects consumed by spiders in unenclosed plots were significantly larger, on average, than were insects consumed by spiders in lizard enclosures and lizard removal enclosures.

**Correct answer: A**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-743-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #744 (test 78, Q6)

**Passage:**

> Manatees, aquatic mammals inhabiting Florida’s rivers and coastal waters, swim close to the surface and are frequently killed in collisions with boats. To address the problem, boat traffic in manatee-populated waters is being required to maintain very low speeds. Unfortunately, manatees are unable to hear low-pitched sounds and a boat’s sound lowers in pitch as the boat slows. Therefore, this approach may in fact make things worse rather than better.

**Question:** Which of the following, if true, casts most doubt on the conclusion?

**Choices:**
- A. Because the water hyacinth that manatees feed on grows best in water that is nearly still, water hyacinth beds can be disturbed or damaged by fast-moving boat traffic.
- B. Over the last several decades, boat traffic in Florida’s coastal waters has been increasing almost continuously and now represents the greatest threat to the endangered manatee population.
- C. The sound of a boat engine generally travels much further under water than it does through the air.
- D. When experimenters exposed manatees to the recorded sounds of boats moving at various speeds, the creatures were unable to discern the sounds over normal background noise.

**Correct answer: D**

### Question #752 (test 79, Q4)

> Several studies of sediment (e.g., dirt, pieces of rock, etc.) in streams have shown an inverse correlation between sediment grain size and downstream distance from the primary sediment source, suggesting that stream length has a sorting effect on sediment. Rather, they concluded that sediment influx in these streams may have been sufficiently spatially diffuse to prevent the typical sorting effect from being observed.

**Question:** Which finding about the streams in the study, if true, would most directly support Litty and Schlunegger’s conclusion?

**Choices:**
- A. The streams contain several types of sediment that are not typically found in streams where the sorting effect has been demonstrated.
- B. The streams are fed by multiple tributaries that carry significant volumes of sediment and that enter the streams downstream of the sampling sites.
- C. The streams mostly originate from the same source, but their lengths vary considerably due to the different courses they take.
- D. The streams regularly experience portions of their banks collapsing into the water at multiple points upstream of the sampling sites.

- C. It would have taken careful effort to make evenly spaced lines on bone with the stone tools typically used by Neanderthals.

**Question:** Which finding, if true, would most directly support the researchers’ hypothesis?

**Choices:**
- A. The LINE transposon in O. vulgaris and O. bimaculoides genomes is active in an octopus brain structure that functions similarly to the human hippocampus.
- B. The human genome contains multiple transposons from the LINE family that are all primarily active in the hippocampus.
- C. A consistent number of copies of LINE transposons is present across the genomes of most octopus species, with few known corruptions.
- D. O. vulgaris and O. bimaculoides have smaller brains than humans do relative to body size, but their genomes contain sequences from a wider variety of transposon families.

**Correct answer: A**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-774-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #775 (test 81, Q7)

**Passage:**

> In the 1980s, many musicians and journalists in the English-speaking world began to draw attention to music from around the globe—such as mbaqanga from South Africa and quan ho from Vietnam—that can’t be easily categorized according to British or North American popular music genres, typically referring to such music as "world music." While some scholars have welcomed this development for bringing diverse musical forms to prominence in countries where they’d previously been overlooked, musicologist Su Zheng claims that the concept of world music homogenizes highly distinct traditions by reducing them all to a single category.

**Question:** Which finding about mbaqanga and quan ho, if true, would most directly support Zheng’s claim?

**Choices:**
- A. Mbaqanga is significantly more popular in the English-speaking world than quan ho is.
- B. Mbaqanga and quan ho developed independently of each other and have little in common musically.
- C. Mbaqanga and quan ho are now performed by a diverse array of musicians with no direct connections to South Africa or Vietnam.
- D. Mbaqanga and quan ho are highly distinct from British and North American popular music genres but similar to each other.

**Correct answer: B**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-775-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #778 (test 81, Q10)

**Passage:**

> Rabbits were introduced to Numa Island in the nineteenth century. Overgrazing by the enormous population of rabbits now menaces the island’s agriculture. The government proposes to reduce the population by using a virus that has caused devastating epidemics in rabbit populations elsewhere. There is, however, a chance that the virus will infect the bilby, an endangered native marsupial. The government’s plan, therefore, may serve the interests of agriculture but will clearly increase the threat to native wildlife.

**Question:** Which of the following, if true, most seriously weakens the argument?

**Choices:**
- A. There is less chance that the virus will infect domestic animals on Numa than that it will infect bilbies.
- B. There are no species of animals on the island that prey on the rabbits.
- C. Overgrazing by rabbits endangers many of the plants on which bilbies feed.
- D. The virus that the government proposes to use has been successfully used elsewhere to control populations of rabbits.

**Correct answer: C**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-778-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #779 (test 82, Q1)

**Passage:**

> In the past, most children who went sledding in the winter snow in Verland used wooden sleds with runners and steering bars. The concern that plastic sleds are more dangerous is clearly borne out by the fact that the number of children injured while sledding was much higher last winter than it was ten years ago.

**Question:** Which of the following, if true in Verland, most seriously undermines the force of the evidence cited?

**Choices:**
- A. A few children still use traditional wooden sleds.
- B. Very few children wear any kind of protective gear, such as helmets, while sledding.
- C. Plastic sleds can be used in a much wider variety of snow conditions than wooden sleds can.
- D. Most sledding injuries occur when a sled collides with a tree, a rock, or, another sled.

**Choices:**
- A. The continuing spread of Eurasian watermilfoil in Frida Lake threatens to choke out the lake’s rare plant species.
- B. Because of ecological conditions prevailing in its native habitat, Eurasian watermilfoil is not as dominant there as it is in Frida Lake.
- C. The proliferation of Eurasian watermilfoil in Frida Lake has led to reductions in the populations of some species of aquatic animals.

### Question #781 (test 82, Q3)

**Passage:**

> Healthy lungs produce a natural antibiotic that protects them from infection by routinely killing harmful bacteria on airway surfaces. People with cystic fibroses, however, are unable to fight off such bacteria, even though their lungs produce normal amounts of the antibiotic. Since the fluid on airway surfaces in the lungs of people with cystic fibrosis bas an abnormally high salt concentration, scientists hypothesize that in high salt environments the antibiotic becomes ineffective at killing harmful bacteria.

**Question:** Which of the following, if it were obtained as an experimental result, would most decisively undermine the scientists’ hypothesis?

**Choices:**
- A. Healthy lungs in which the salt concentration of the airway-surface fluid has been substantially increased are able to reestablish their normal salt concentration within a relatively short period of time.
- B. The antibiotic produced by the lungs is effective at killing harmful bacteria even when salt concentrations are below levels typical of healthy lungs.
- C. The salt concentration of the airway-surface fluid in the lungs of people who suffer from cystic fibrosis tends to return to its former high levels after having been reduced to levels typical of healthy lungs.
- D. The lungs of people who suffer from cystic fibrosis are unable to fight off harmful bacteria even when the salt concentration is reduced to levels typical of healthy lungs.

**Correct answer: D**

## Command of Evidence: Quantitative (55)

### Question #855 (test 90, Q2)

**Passage:**

> Ablation Rates for Three Elements in Cosmic Dust, by Dust Source
> Earth’s atmosphere is bombarded by cosmic dust originating from several sources： short- period comets (SPCs), particles from the asteroid belt (ASTs), Halley-type comets (HTCs), and Oort cloud comets (OCCs). Some of the dust’s material vaporizes in the atmosphere in a process called ablation, and the faster the particles move, the higher the rate of ablation. Astrophysicist Juan Diego Carrillo-Sanchez led a team that calculated average ablation rates for elements in the dust (such as iron and potassium) and showed that material in slower-moving SPC or AST dust has a lower rate than the same material in faster-moving HTC or OCC dust. For example, whereas the average ablation rate for iron from AST dust is 28%, the average rate for  ________ .

**Choices:**
- A. iron from SPC dust is 20%
- B. sodium from OCC dust is 100%.
- C. iron from HTC dust is 90%.
- D. sodium from AST dust is 75%

**Correct answer: C**

### Question #859 (test 90, Q6)

### Question #865 (test 91, Q2)

**Passage:**

> Average Number and Duration of Torpor Bouts and Arousal Episodes for Alaska Marmots and Arctic Ground Squirrels, 2008-2011
> When hibernating, Alaska marmots and Arctic ground squirrels enter a state called torpor, which minimizes the energy their bodies need to function. Often a hibernating animal will temporarily come out of torpor (called an arousal episode) and its metabolic rate will rise, burning more of the precious energy the animal needs to survive the winter. Alaska marmots hibernate in groups and therefore burn less energy keeping warm during these episodes than they would if they were alone. A researcher hypothesized that because Arctic ground squirrels hibernate alone, they would likely exhibit longer bouts of torpor and shorter arousal episodes than Alaska marmots.

- C. The Alaska marmots had shorter torpor bouts and longer arousal episodes than the Arctic ground squirrels did.
- D. The Alaska marmots had more torpor bouts than arousal episodes, but their arousal episodes were much shorter than their torpor bouts.

**Correct answer: C**

> Political scientist Todd Hartman demonstrated how metaphors matter by applying them to the rather arcane policy issue of network neutrality. The issue affects how information is priced and transmitted over the Internet, and became important in response to lobbying efforts by broadband service providers to be allowed to charge a premium for transmitting certain data at high speeds. To see whether metaphors could move people more than straightforward, unembellished language, Hartman had some volunteers in his study to read this accurate, but somewhat dry statement. Others got a version that was built around the metaphor of the Internet as the information superhighway. Not surprisingly, the persuasive effects of the two messages were dramatically different. Among the group who’d read the message laced with metaphor, the level of support for network neutrality soared to 61%.

- B. Students with a high level of political sophistication tend to have a more positive attitude toward network neutrality when it’s explained to them in a literal message because the conciseness of this type of message helps keep their attention.
- C. Students with a low level of political sophistication tend to have a more positive attitude toward network neutrality when it’s explained to them in a literal message because this type of message more clearly explains the facts surrounding the issue.
- D. Students with a low level of political sophistication tend to have a more negative attitude toward network neutrality when it’s explained to them in a literal message because this type of message is the least likely to help listeners form an opinion.

The conclusion was clear: over the course of a century, plants had adapted to the conditions they experienced on their own subplots.

### Question #907 (test 95, Q4)

**Passage:**

> The Tsangpo Gorge in Tibet, one of the deepest canyons in the world, formed when tectonic forces pushed up the earth and steepened the path of a river that then caused massive erosion. Dirk Scherler, a geologist at the German Research Centre for Geosciences in Potsdam, and his colleagues said they think an unknown event caused an increase in tectonic uplift rates about 3 million years ago. As the uplift became more pronounced, about 2.5 million years ago, it dammed the Yarlung Tsangpo River that runs through the region, preventing it from flowing down the mountains. Once the river dropped enough sediment and made it over the natural dam, it quickly flowed down the mountain. At that point, the mountain was steeper because of the increased tectonic uplift, causing the river to run faster and leading to vast erosion in the gorge.

**Question:** The data in figure, gathered by Scherler’s team, indicate that the team estimated the Yarlung Tsangpo Rivers valley floor before uplift to be a

**Choices:**
- A. steep drop followed by a gradual slope.
- B. series of erratic elevation changes.
- C. flat plane between gentle inclines.
- D. steadily decreasing slope

**Correct answer: D**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-907-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #908 (test 95, Q5)

**Passage:**

> As more of the economy becomes automated, doomsayers worry that the gap between the haves and the have-nots will only grow. Compiling records from churches, monasteries, colleges, guilds and governments, Gregory Clark, an economist at the University of California, Davis, has put together a comprehensive dataset of English wages that stretches back to the 13th century. The first drop came in the 14th century, and had nothing to do with technological change. Life expectancy in medieval England was short and interest rates were high, meaning that taking on the seven-year apprenticeship needed to become a craftsman came with a heavy opportunity cost. But interest rates started falling in this period, from around 10% in 1290 to 7.5% in 1340. When the Black Death struck England in 1348, wiping out a third of the population, interest rates fell further, to 5%, and apprenticeships became much more attractive. The increased supply of skilled labour relative to unskilled workers drove down the wage premium.

**Question:** Based on Clark’s findings, as described in the text, what is the most likely explanation for the data for 1200-1300 shown in the graph?

**Choices:**
- A. Life expectancy rose, so more workers became apprentices, which increased the supply of skilled workers.
- B. Interest rates rose, so fewer workers became apprentices, which decreased the supply of skilled workers.
- C. Cities were growing at a rapid rate, which resulted in projects that increased the demand for unskilled workers.
- D. New inventions were automating complex tasks, which decreased the demand for skilled workers.

**Correct answer: B**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-908-answer-and-explanation.html_

To the researchers’ surprise, though, the data indicated that during this period,  ________

**Question:** Which choice most effectively uses data from the graph to complete the sentence?

**Choices:**
- A. the most abundant mysticete species had a typical length of only about 6 meters.
- B. nearly half of mysticete species had a typical length of less than 5 meters.
- C. the largest mysticete species had a typical length of only about 12 meters.
- D. most mysticete species had a typical length of less than 5 meters.

**Correct answer: D**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-932-answer-and-explanation.html_

- A. the use of fire might have originated independently in several different parts of the world

- C. the charred rocks found in the deposit included several different kinds of stone, all of which are common in the immediate vicinity of the site
- D. it is impossible to determine whether a charred deposit dating from so long ago resulted from a fire caused by lightening or from the purposeful use of fire

**Correct answer: D**

- B. Whitten's work from the 1960s exhibits many more gestures than his work from the 1970s does.
- C. Whitten became less interested in exploring the role of gesture in his work as his career progressed.
- D. Whitten's work from the 1960s is much more realistic than his work from the 1970s is.

**Correct answer: B**

### Question #1283 (test 135, Q7)

**Passage:**

> Biologist Natacha Bodenhausen and colleagues analyzed the naturally occurring bacterial communities associated with leaves and roots of wild Arabidopsis thaliana, a small flowering plant. The researchers found many of the same bacterial genera in both the plants' leaves and roots. To explain this, the researchers pointed to the general proximity of A. thaliana leaves to the ground and noted that rain splashing off soil could bring soil-based bacteria into contact with the leaves. Alternatively, the researchers noted that wind, which may be a source of bacteria in the aboveground portion of plants, could also bring bacteria to the soil and roots. Either explanation suggests that  ________

**Question:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

**Choices:**
- A. A. thaliana leaves and roots are especially vulnerable to harmful bacteria.
- B. bacteria carried by wind are typically less beneficial to A. thaliana than soil-based bacteria are.
- C. many bacteria in A. thaliana leaves may have been deposited by means other than rain.
- D. some bacteria in A. thaliana leaves and roots may share a common source.

**Correct answer: D**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-1283-answer-and-explanation.html_

While some women in San Francisco's Chinatown enlisted in the armed services, many others went to work in businesses outside their neighborhood and in defense industries for the first time.

**Question:** Which choice best characterizes the overall structure of the text?

> Animal live longer when their calorie intake is restricted to two-thirds of what is considered normal for their species. Indeed, however, calorie restriction does not slow mammalian metabolism, and in yeast and worms, metabolism is both sped up and altered.

### Question #1100 (test 116, Q6)

**Passage:**

> In the novel "The Great Gatsby" by F. Scott Fitzgerald, the character Jay Gatsby is portrayed as an enigmatic figure, with his true identity and past shrouded in mystery.

**Question:** Which quotation from the novel best supports this characterization of Gatsby?

**Choices:**
- A. "I am the son of some wealthy people in the Middle West—all dead now. I was brought up in America but educated at Oxford, because all my ancestors have been educated there for many years. It is a family tradition."
- B. "He smiled understandingly—much more than understandingly. It was one of those rare smiles with a quality of eternal reassurance in it, that you may come across four or five times in life. It faced—or seemed to face—the whole external world for an instant, and then concentrated on you with an irresistible prejudice in your favor. It understood you just as far as you wanted to be understood, believed in you as you would like to believe in yourself."
- C. "I told you I went there [Oxford]. I heard the name for the first time just now. It was the one thing I didn't disclose. Don't you think I was brought up for a gentleman?"
- D. "I'm sorry about the clock," he said. "It's an old clock," I told him idiotically. I think we all believed for a moment that it had smashed in pieces on the floor."

**Correct answer: B**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-1100-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #1101 (test 116, Q7)

**Passage:**

> In the poem "The Waste Land" by T.S. Eliot, the speaker reflects on the fragmentation and disillusionment of modern society.

**Question:** Which quotation from the poem best supports this claim?

**Choices:**
- A. "April is the cruelest month, breeding / Lilacs out of the dead land, mixing / Memory and desire, stirring / Dull roots with spring rain."
- B. "I will show you fear in a handful of dust."
- C. "The eyes are not here / There are no eyes here / In this valley of dying stars / In this hollow valley / This broken jaw of our lost kingdoms."
- D. "Unreal city / Under the brown fog of a winter dawn, / A crowd flowed over London Bridge, so many, / I had not thought death had undone so many."

**Correct answer: A**

### Question #1102 (test 116, Q8)

**Passage:**

> According to a recent survey on online education, some students find it difficult to learn effectively in a virtual classroom because they are accustomed to the structure and social interaction of a traditional classroom environment. The survey conducted interviews with a range of students across different age groups and found that some struggled with the lack of face-to-face interaction and felt disconnected learning online.

**Question:** Which quotation from the survey best supports this claim?

**Choices:**
- A. "I enjoy online learning because I can work at my own pace and have more flexibility in my schedule. However, I do miss being in a physical classroom and interacting with my classmates."
- B. "Virtual classrooms have been a great alternative for me. I appreciate the convenience and the ability to review the material as many times as I need to."
- C. "Online learning is ideal for students who are introverted and prefer to work independently. It gives me the freedom to complete my work on my own terms."
- D. "I found it difficult to stay motivated and engaged during online classes. There were times when I felt isolated and disconnected from my teachers and classmates."

**Correct answer: D**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-1102-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #1104 (test 116, Q10)

**Passage:**

> Pulitzer Prize–winning writer Héctor Tobar has built a multifaceted career as both a journalist and an author of short stories and novels. In an essay about Tobar's work, a student claims that Tobar blends his areas of expertise by applying journalism techniques to his creation of works of fiction.

**Question:** Which quotation from a literary critic best supports the student's claim?

**Choices:**
- A. "For one novel, an imagined account of a real person's global travels, Tobar approached his subject like a reporter, interviewing people the man had met along the way and researching the man's own writings."
- B. "Tobar got his start as a volunteer for El Tecolote, a community newspaper in San Francisco, and wrote for newspapers for years before earning a degree in creative writing and starting to publish works of fiction."
- C. "Many of Tobar's notable nonfiction articles are marked by the writer's use of techniques usually associated with fiction, such as complex narrative structures and the incorporation of symbolism."
- D. "The protagonist of Tobar's third novel is a man who wants to be a novelist and keeps notes about interesting people he encounters so he can use them when developing characters for his stories."

**Correct answer: A**

### Question #1110 (test 117, Q6)

**Passage:**

> The Catcher in the Rye is a 1951 novel by J.D Salinger. In the novel, Salinger portrays Holden Caulfield as a disillusioned teenager who feels alienated from the society around him.

**Question:** Which quotation from The Catcher in the Rye most effectively illustrates this claim?

**Choices:**
- A. "I'm the most terrific liar you ever saw in your life. It's terrible. If I'm on my way to the store to buy a magazine, even, and somebody asks me where I'm going, I'm liable to say I'm going to the opera. It's terrible."
- B. "I'm standing on the edge of some crazy cliff. What I have to do, I have to catch everybody if they start to go over the cliff--I mean if they're running and they don't look where they're going I have to come out from somewhere and catch them. That's all I do all day."
- C. "I didn't want to tell anybody anything. I'm a madman," I said. I really was. "I'm a madman.""
- D. "I'm sick of just liking people. I wish to God I could meet somebody I could respect."

**Correct answer: B**

### Question #1111 (test 117, Q7)

> In "I Hear America Singing," Walt Whitman suggests that his readers have not fully appreciated the diversity of the American people, writing, "Each singing what belongs to him or her and to none else, / The day what belongs to the day—at night the party of young fellows, robust, friendly, / Singing with open mouths their strong melodious songs.  ________ "

- C. "The boatman singing what belongs to him in his boat—the deckhand singing on the steamboat deck,"
- D. "The delicious singing of the mother—or of the young wife at work—or of the girl sewing or washing— / Each singing what belongs to her, and to none else."

### Question #1116 (test 118, Q2)

> Electra is a circa 420–410 BCE play by Sophocles, translated in 1870 by R.C. Jebb. Electra, who is in mourning for her dead father and her long-absent brother, is aware of the intensity of her grief but believes it to be justified:  ________

**Question:** Which quotation from Electra most effectively illustrates the claim?

- B. "I know my own passion, it escapes me not; but, seeing that the causes are so dire, will never curb these frenzied plaints, while life is in me."
- C. "Send to me my brother; for I have no more the strength to bear up alone against the load of grief that weighs me down."
- D. "But never will I cease from dirge and sore lament, while I look on the trembling rays of the bright stars, or on this light of day."

The novel is set in the fictional town of Maycomb, Alabama during the Great Depression and follows the story of a young girl named Scout Finch as she grows up and learns about the racial inequality and prejudice that exists in her community. The novel explores themes of racism, justice, morality, and the loss of innocence. It has become a classic of modern American literature and is widely regarded as one of the greatest novels of the 20th century.

**Question:** Which quotation best illustrates the prejudice and racism in Maycomb County?

**Choices:**
- A. "There's something in our world that makes men lose their heads—they couldn't be fair if they tried. In our courts, when it's a white man's word against a black man's, the white man always wins."
- B. "I never loved reading until I feared I would lose it. One does not love breathing."
- C. "People generally see what they look for, and hear what they listen for."
- D. "Until I feared I would lose it, I never loved to read. One does not love breathing."

**Correct answer: A**

### Question #1122 (test 118, Q8)

**Passage:**

> In The Scarlet Letter, Nathaniel Hawthorne portrays Hester Prynne as having a deep emotional connection to her daughter Pearl.

**Question:** Which quotation from the novel most effectively illustrates this claim?

**Choices:**
- A. "The scarlet letter was her passport into regions where other women dared not tread. Shame, Despair, Solitude! These had been her teachers—stern and wild ones—and they had made her strong, but taught her much amiss."
- B. "She had wandered, without rule or guidance, in a moral wilderness, as vast, as intricate and shadowy, as the untamed forest, amid the gloom of which they were now holding a colloquy that was to decide their fate."
- C. "Pearl was a born outcast of the infantile world. An imp of evil, emblem and product of sin, she had no right among christened infants."
- D. "But she named the infant ‘Pearl,' as being of great price—purchased with all she had—her mother's only treasure!"

### Question #1125 (test 119, Q2)

**Passage:**

> Sense and Sensibility is an 1811 novel by Jane Austen. In the novel, Austen describes Marianne Dashwood's ability to persuade others of the rightness of her artistic judgments, as is evident when Marianne visits with John Willoughby, a potential suitor:  ________

**Question:** Which quotation from Sense and Sensibility most effectively illustrates the claim?

**Choices:**
- A. "Above all, when she heard him declare, that of music and dancing he was passionately fond, she gave him such a look of approbation as secured the largest share of his discourse to herself for the rest of his stay."
- B. "Their taste was strikingly alike. The same books, the same passages were idolized by each—or if any difference appeared, any objection arose, it lasted no longer than till the force of her arguments and the brightness of her eyes could be displayed."
- C. "It was only necessary to mention any favourite amusement to engage her to talk. She could not be silent when such points were introduced, and she had neither shyness nor reserve in their discussion."
- D. "They speedily discovered that their enjoyment of dancing and music was mutual, and that it arose from a general conformity of judgment in all that related to either. Encouraged by this to a further examination of his opinions, she proceeded to question him on the subject of books."

**Correct answer: B**

### Question #1131 (test 119, Q8)

**Passage:**

> Human activity has greatly accelerated the rate at which plant and animal species are becoming established in ecosystems beyond their native habitats, sometimes with harmful consequences for native species. Theory suggests that ecosystems with high species diversity should be more resistant to such invasions by nonnative species than less diverse ecosystems are. However, a study of a California wetland system found that the most diverse ecosystems are actually the most invaded by nonnative plants.

**Question:** Which choice best describes the overall structure  of the text?

**Choices:**
- A. The text discusses the relationship between species diversity and nonnative species invasions, presenting a theory and then providing an example that contradicts that theory.
- B. The text discusses the importance of nonnative species invasions in California wetlands, then provides a theory about how species diversity affects these invasions.
- C. It describes the consequences of human activity on plant and animal species, then presents a study about the impact of nonnative species invasions on ecosystems.
- D. The text explains how nonnative species invasions can harm native species, then provides a theory about the importance of efficient resource use for ecosystem resistance.

**Correct answer: A**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-1131-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #1132 (test 119, Q9)

**Passage:**

> In recent decades, scholars of American literature have skillfully revealed authors' simultaneous accommodation and resistance to * an increasingly commercialized, capitalistic environment * during the early nineteenth century. Historians of the period have not, however, fully exploited literary criticism, due to the disciplinary boundaries that * mark * contemporary academic research. Few historians have extensive training in critical theory and its specialized languages, and the sheer volume of work in early American history and literature challenges anyone who would master either field, much less both.

**Question:** Which choice best describes the overall structure  of the text?

**Choices:**
- A. The text discusses the intersection between American literature and history, highlighting the lack of collaboration between scholars in both fields.
- B. The text analyzes the differences between American and British literature during the early nineteenth century, providing examples from various authors.
- C. It discusses the role of commercialism in American literature, arguing that authors fully embraced it in their works.
- D. It argues that historians have not fully utilized literary criticism in their research, but have instead focused on other disciplines.

**Correct answer: A**

### Question #622 (test 65, Q6)

**Passage:**

> The following text is from Countee Cullen’s 1919 poem "For a Lady I Know." Countee Cullen is a prominent African American poet.
> She even thinks that up in heaven
> Her class lies late and snores
> While poor black cherubs rise at seven
> To do celestial chores.

**Question:** Which choice best states the main purpose of the text?

**Choices:**
- A. To protest against child labour
- B. To protest against gender inequality
- C. To protest against slavery in the South
- D. To protest against social class discrimination

**Correct answer: D**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-622-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #623 (test 65, Q7)

**Passage:**

> The following text is from Alfred Edward Housman’s 1896 poem "When I Was One-and-Twenty."
> When I was one-and-twenty
> I heard a wise man say,
> "Give crowns and pounds and guineas
> But not your heart away;
> Give pearls away and rubies
> But keep your fancy free."
> But I was one-and-twenty,
> No use to talk to me.
> When I was one-and-twenty
> I heard him say again,
> "The heart out of the bosom
> Was never given in vain;
> "Tis paid with sighs a plenty
> And sold for endless rue."
> And I am two-and-twenty,
> And oh, ‘tis true, ‘tis true.

**Question:** Which choice best states the main purpose of the text?

**Choices:**
- A. To explore the easy mistakes in one’s love life, despite knowing exactly what should be done
- B. To persuade the readers to embrace another wise person’s advice on their love life
- C. To recommend the readers to listen to others not to regret their choices later
- D. To tell a young man’s mistakes in love, despite the produced recommendation

**Correct answer: B**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-623-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #625 (test 65, Q9)

**Passage:**

> The following text is adapted from Herman Melville’s 1857 novel The Confidence-Man. Humphry Davy was a prominent British chemist and inventor.

Great was the savant’s disdain; but, chancing ere long to find himself in a corner with the jackanapes, got into conversation with him, when he was somewhat ill-prepared for the good sense of the jackanapes, but was altogether thrown aback, upon subsequently being [informed that he was] no less a personage than Sir Humphry Davy.

### Question #627 (test 66, Q2)

**Passage:**

> The following text is from Countee Cullen’s 1927 poem "From the Dark Tower."

> The golden increment of bursting fruit,
> Not always countenance, abject and mute,
> That lesser men should hold their brothers cheap;

> Shall we beguile their limbs with mellow flute,

### Question #628 (test 66, Q3)

**Passage:**

> The following text is from Claude McKay’s 1919 poem "If We Must Die." Claude McKay is a Jamaican poet.
> If we must die, let it not be like hogs
> Hunted and penned in an inglorious spot,
> While round us bark the mad and hungry dogs,
> Making their mock at our accursèd lot.
> If we must die, O let us nobly die,
> So that our precious blood may not be shed
> In vain; then even the monsters we defy
> Shall be constrained to honor us though dead!
> O kinsmen! we must meet the common foe!
> Though far outnumbered let us show us brave,
> And for their thousand blows deal one death-blow!
> What though before us lies the open grave?
> Like men we’ll face the murderous, cowardly pack,
> Pressed to the wall, dying, but fighting back!

**Question:** Which choice best states the main purpose of the text?

**Choices:**
- A. To call for Black individuals to resist anti-Black racism
- B. To propose a radical solution to the suffering of the oppressed people
- C. To argue that violent acts of resistance are the only viable option for these oppressed individuals
- D. To encourage the oppressed people to bravely resist their oppressors and fight for their rights and freedom, even if they die in the struggle

**Correct answer: D**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-628-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #633 (test 66, Q8)

**Passage:**

> I had grown up in the United States virtually without relatives, which, in my intense desire to assimilate, was quite all right with me. But this attitude dissolved when I walked into that apartment in Beijing. I realized then that my extended family is not just a collection of accidental alliances but a living body, an entity that will welcome me for being simply who I am: the daughter of my mother, the niece of my aunts and uncles. We had never before seen each other but, in that moment, we shared a sense of connection and loyalty unlike anything I had previously experienced.

**Question:** The primary purpose of the passage is to

**Choices:**
- A. describe the author’s travels to Beijing
- B. reveal how lonely the author often felt

- D. illustrate the closeness that existed among the author’s mother, aunts, and uncles

### Question #634 (test 66, Q9)

**Passage:**

> For people with synesthesia, a condition in which the senses get mixed up, the number five may be red. One explanation for this sensory crossover is that synesthetes, people with synesthesia, are simply experiencing childhood memories and associations. Maybe the person played with refrigerator magnets as a child and the number five was red. Another explanation is that synesthetes are experiencing the result of some kind of cross wiring between regions of the brain. In fact, we have now identified where in the brain such cross wiring might occur. We have also determined that synesthesia can occur if the wiring is fine but the balance of chemicals that control brain activity is skewed.

**Question:** The primary purpose of the passage is to

**Choices:**
- A. explore possible explanations for a phenomenon
- B. compare two somewhat related phenomena
- C. trace the evolution of an intriguing phenomenon
- D. discuss an experiment that illustrates an uncommon phenomenon

**Correct answer: A**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-634-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #637 (test 67, Q3)

**Passage:**

> As a slang word "cool" has stayed cool far longer than most such words. One of the main characteristics of slang is the continual renewal of its vocabulary: in order for slang to feel slangy, it has to have a feeling of novelty. Slang expressions meaning the same thing as "cool," like "groovy," "hep," "far-out," "rad," and "tubular," have for the most part not had the staying power of "cool." In general, there is no intrinsic reason why one word stays alive and others get consigned to the scrap heap of linguistic history, but slang terms, like fashion designs, are rarely "in" for long. The jury is still out on how long "def" and "phat" will survive.

**Question:** The primary purpose of the passage is to

**Choices:**
- A. address a pressing question
- B. define an unusual expression
- C. note the durability of a term
- D. oppose a particular use of language

**Correct answer: C**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-637-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #638 (test 67, Q4)

**Passage:**

> We pay a steep price for our reliance on high yields and shallow roots, says soil scientist—and National Geographic emerging explorer—Jerry Glover of the Land Institute in the United States. Because annual root crops mostly tap into only the top foot or so of soil, that layer gets depleted, forcing farmers to rely on large amounts of fertilizers to maintain high yields. Annuals also promote heavy use of pesticides or tillage because they leave the ground bare much of the year. Th at allows weeds to invade.

**Question:** Which choice best states the main idea of the text?

**Choices:**
- A. famers usually get a high yield from perennial crops.
- B. annual crops can be very costly to grow year after year.
- C. conservation practices have reduced the rate of soil loss in the United States.
- D. perennial crops may grow better than annual crops in Nepal’s harsh climate.

**Correct answer: B**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-673-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #678 (test 71, Q8)

**Passage:**

> NASA’s Cassini probe has detected an unusual wobble in the rotation of Mimas, Saturn’s smallest moon. Using a computer model to study Mimas’s gravitational interactions with Saturn and tidal forces, geophysicist Alyssa Rhoden and colleagues have proposed that this wobble could be due to a liquid ocean moving beneath the moon’s icy surface. The researchers believe other moons should be examined to see if they too might have oceans hidden beneath their surfaces.

**Question:** Which choice best states the main idea of the text?

**Choices:**
- A. Rhoden and colleagues were the first to confirm that several of Saturn’s moons contain hidden oceans.
- B. Research has failed to identify signs that there is an ocean hidden beneath the surface of Mimas.
- C. Rhoden and colleagues created a new computer model that identifies moons with hidden oceans without needing to analyze the moons’ rotation.
- D. Research has revealed that an oddity in the rotation of Mimas could be explained by an ocean hidden beneath its surface.

**Correct answer: D**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-678-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #679 (test 71, Q9)

**Passage:**

> To protect themselves when being attacked, hagfish—jawless marine animals that resemble eels—will release large quantities of slimy, mucus-like threads. - C. Hagfish have inspired researchers to develop a new petroleum-based fabric.
- D. The slimy threads that hagfish release might help researchers create a new kind of fabric.

**Correct answer: D**

### Question #680 (test 72, Q1)

**Passage:**

> According to a recent study on the effects of social media on mental health, excessive use of social media has been linked to increased levels of anxiety and depression in young adults. The study, which surveyed over 1,000 individuals between the ages of 18 and 30, found that those who spent more than two hours per day on social media were more likely to report symptoms of anxiety and depression.

**Question:** Which of the following best describes the structure of the text?

**Choices:**
- A. The text presents a claim about social media use and mental health, and then discusses the results of a study that supports this claim.
- B. The text presents a claim about anxiety and depression, and then discusses the benefits of social media use.
- C. The text presents a claim about the negative effects of social media, and then discusses the importance of social media in modern society.
- D. The text presents a claim about mental health, and then discusses the history of social media and its impact on society.

**Correct answer: A**

### Question #681 (test 72, Q2)

**Passage:**

> Morning people, cheerfully referred to as early birds, set the attitudinal tone of the entire culture. They’ve decided that morning is the best time of the day, and that night owls ( people who are not naturally bright eyed and bushy tailed at the very crack of dawn ) are in some way lazy and unfocused. In my view, morning is not the best time of day. It’s a fine time of day, of course, but it isn’t inherently better than any other time. Getting up early does not necessarily mean you’re more productive or a better member of society. I tried living on my husband’s schedule, and all I did was sleep through my most productive hours.

**Question:** Which best describes the function of the parenthetical statement?

**Choices:**
- A. to offer an observation
- B. to suggest an alternative
- C. to underscore a preference
- D. to define a term

**Correct answer: D**

### Question #688 (test 72, Q9)

**Passage:**

> The novelist Jane Austen (1775-1817) wrote about what she understood. But did she understand enough? Her world was not merely small but narrow. Her novels are sometimes referred to as miniatures, but the analogy is not apt. We do not get from Emma a condensed and refined sense of a larger entity, nor does the work suggest references far beyond its surface. The limitation and the narrowness of the small English rural towns her novels are often set in is the limitation of class society. And the on important criticism of Jane Austen is that her vision is limited by her unquestioning acceptance of that society.

**Question:** The author indicates that Austen’s novels cannot be considered "miniatures" because they

**Choices:**

- B. develop highly complex plots
- C. do not convey a sense of the world at large
- D. do not distinguish between social classes

**Correct answer: C**

### Question #689 (test 72, Q10)

**Passage:**

> The phenomenon of simultaneous discovery–what science historians call "multiples"–turns out to be extremely common. For Ogburn and Thomas, the sheer number of multiples could mean only one thing: scientific discoveries must, in some sense, be inevitable. They must be in the air, products of the intellectual climate of a specific time and place.

- B. validate a statement

- D. downplay an outcome

### Question #690 (test 73, Q1)

**Passage:**

> Archaeologists have traditionally thought that the rise of agriculture required early farmers to settle down near their crops. But new findings suggest that Catalhoyük, Turkey was smack in the middle of marshy wetlands. Archaeologist Arlene Rosen’s analysis of fossil remains of wheat and barley found at Catalhoyük indicates that the grain was grown in a dry area. Some experts reject the implication that Catalhoyük’s farmers cultivated distant fields, since large quantities of grain would have had to be transported. However, archaeobotanist Eleni Asouti has shown that the wood used for construction at Catalhoyük grew at least twelve kilometers away from the village.

**Question:** According to the text, why the author mentions Asouti’s research?

**Choices:**
- A. undermines the claim that the villagers somehow transported materials across distances
- B. reinforces archaeologists’ traditional view of the rise of agriculture
- C. provides support for the view that Catalhoyük’s farmers could have cultivated distant fields
- D. qualifies Rosen’s theories about the Catalhoyük Farmers

**Correct answer: B**

_Source: https://www.crackap.com/sat/reading-writing/question-690-answer-and-explanation.html_

### Question #708 (test 74, Q9)

### Question #709 (test 74, Q10)

**Passage:**

> Duke Ellington considered himself "the world’s greatest listener." In music, hearing is all. Judging by the two or three thousand pieces of music Ellington wrote, he could probably hear a flea scratching itself and put that rhythm into one of his compositions.  For him the sounds of the world were the ingredients he mixed into appetizers, main courses, and desserts to satisfy the appetite of his worldwide audience, He wasn’t averse to going out in a boat to catch the fish himself, He would raise the fowl himself. But when that musical meat appeared before you none of the drudgery showed.

**Question:** The author most likely refers to the "flea" in order to

**Choices:**
- A. highlight Ellington’s prodigious memory
- B. emphasize the quality of Ellington’s listening skills.
- C. suggest that Ellington’s compositions were marked by rhythmic similarities
- D. imply that Ellington could be overly concerned about minutia

**Correct answer: B**

### Question #710 (test 75, Q1)

**Passage:**

> During the early development of a new idea, scientists behave rather like artists, driven by temperament. We start off with a hunch, a feeling even a desire that the world be one way, and then we proceed from that presentiment often sticking with it long after the data suggest we may be leading ourselves and others down a blind alley. What saves us is that experiment acts as the ultimate referee, settling all disputes. No matter how strong our hunch is, at some point we will have to prove it with facts. Otherwise our hunch, no matter how strongly held, will remain just that.

**Question:** Which choice best describes the use of the reference to "the ultimate referee" in the text?

- B. to emphasize the need for objectivity
- C. to stress the need for commitment
- D. to emphasize the need for artistry

### Question #714 (test 75, Q5)

**Passage:**

> For decades studies of self-control in short-term decision- making have led to two clear, but seemingly contradictory, results. Part of the problem has been how hard it is to conduct behavioral research. Traditional methods assume that test subjects fully understand the Questions they’re asked and give honest answers. Unfortunately, researchers had no practical way of knowing whether this was the case, or whether they actually measured what they intended to. But here at the nation’s largest biometrics lab, Texas A&M colleagues and Mr. Kang figured out a new way to investigate the question that didn’t rely on just what volunteers report to us.

**Question:** Based on the passage, the author would most likely agree that self-control studies prior to the one he conducted with his colleagues had which problem?

**Choices:**
- A. The studies relied in part on data that may have been inaccurate, and thus the studies may have overstated or understated participants’ self-control.
- B. The studies typically used methods that exhausted participants, and thus the studies may have made participants appear to have less self-control than they actually did.
- C. The studies were designed in ways that may have encouraged participants to show more self-control than the participants would show in nonexperimental settings.
- D. The studies tended to include participants who were not representative of the populations being studied with regard to their level of self-control.

**Correct answer: A**

### Question #721 (test 76, Q2)

**Passage:**

> For decades studies of self-control in short-term decision- making have led to two clear, but seemingly contradictory, results. One model suggested that self-control is a finite resource that can get used up if you lean on it too heavily, like a battery that loses its charge over time. Each little demonstration of self-control throughout the day ends up exhausting the limited reserves. The alternative model suggested that exercising self-control can help you build up the skill like a snowball that gets bigger as it builds momentum rolling downhill.

**Question:** In the paragraph, the author refers to a battery and a snowball most likely to

**Choices:**
- A. support the validity of behavioral models by showing the validity of analogous models.
- B. emphasize the contrast between psychological theories by presenting a familiar natural contrast.
- C. identify similarities between two accounts of motivation that are typically regarded as distinct.
- D. clarify hypothesized mental processes by comparing them to accessible physical processes.
